<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01V4hcNTyR161s3jFuAUp6QD`</summary>

```text
1	\section{When Does Discriminability Fail as Reward Utility?}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Root cause of the probe reward trap.}
4	The probe reward trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) and the cosine failures (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}) share a common geometric root.
5	$\dsyc$ encodes the \emph{direction} of belief change but not its \emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.
6	The two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.
7	Cosine $\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\dsyc$.
8	The binary probe $\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.
9	These are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).
10	Cross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779 \pm 0.019$ on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.
11	This pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.
12	\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable.
13	A quantitative comparison of geometric separability between $\dsyc$ and their reward-hacking directions remains an important open question.
14	Independently, \citet{kelkar2026persona} find that persona vectors are geometrically independent of $\dsyc$ in activation space, further suggesting that $\dsyc$ does not encode the full semantic structure of sycophancy.
15	The multi-dimensional decomposition of sycophancy into independently steerable sub-behaviors~\citep{vennemeyer2025sycophancy} compounds this limitation: $\dsyc$ projects distinct sub-phenomena onto a single axis, amplifying the warranted-vs-unwarranted ambiguity.
16	For sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.
17	
18	\paragraph{GRPO results under our configuration.}
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\tof$ and $\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \textbf{bold}. $^\dagger$Best checkpoint (seed 1, step 6500); only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\ddagger$Seed 2 (15 checkpoints); all exceed vanilla. Best $\sycon{=}0.286$ (step 7000). $^\S$All cosine variants collapsed from step 1 ($\tof{=}0$ at every checkpoint); F1d additionally diverged at step 2160 (entropy ${<}\,0.01$). $^\P$ACT and ActAdd differ from GRPO along 5+ dimensions (\S\ref{sec:discussion}); included for context, not as controlled comparisons. Under arithmetic mean $(\tof{+}\nof)/2$, qualitative rankings are preserved but collapsed methods (0.498--0.500) appear indistinguishable from vanilla (0.509), masking behavioral death; harmonic mean's zero-floor on $\tof{=}0$ is by design.}
23	\label{tab:main_comparison}
24	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
25	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}
26	\toprule
27	Method & Type & $\sycon$ $\uparrow$ & $\tof$ $\uparrow$ & $\nof$ $\uparrow$ & Step \\
28	\midrule
29	Vanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\
30	\midrule
31	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{GRPO with $\dsyc$}} \\
32	Cosine $\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & 0.000$^\S$ & 0.000 & 1.000 & 500 \\
33	Continuous Probe $\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\
34	\midrule
35	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{SFT with $\dsyc$ probe penalty}} \\
36	Binary Probe $\rone$ (F2) & SFT & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\
37	\midrule
38	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{GRPO without $\dsyc$}} \\
39	$\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.153$^\dagger$ & 0.083 & 0.983 & 6500 \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{Reference methods (not directly comparable)$^\P$}} \\
42	ActAdd ($\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\
43	\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \textbf{0.301} & \textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\
44	ACT (seed 2)$^\ddagger$ & SFT & 0.286 & -- & -- & 7000 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	If the cosine and probe failures (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) were caused by $\dsyc$ alone, removing it should recover calibration.
50	$\rthree$-only GRPO tests this hypothesis by relying solely on the NLI behavioral reward---and fails: only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla (14.3\%), with both seeds oscillating ($\Delta \approx 0.069$) before diverging (Table~\ref{tab:main_comparison}; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}).
51	The failure is not representational: $\dsyc$ AUROC varies by ${<}0.01$ across checkpoints and $\rthree$ saturates at ${\sim}0.98$ ($p{=}0.703$), yet $\sycon$ swings by 17\%.
52	This disconnect between stable representations and unstable behavior points to the per-completion reward structure, not the reward content, as the bottleneck.
53	
54	\paragraph{Competing hypotheses and ablation.}
55	Three explanations are consistent with these failures: (a)~$\dsyc$ is fundamentally unsuitable as a sycophancy reward; (b)~group size$=$2 provides insufficient ranking diversity; (c)~sycophancy calibration resists scalar per-completion RL.
56	We designed two ablations to discriminate among them (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}).
57	\emph{Group-size ablation.}
58	Per-completion GRPO fails at both group sizes 2 and 8; the gs$=$8 trajectory ($0.084 \to 0.085 \to 0.054$, all below vanilla; terminated early) rules out insufficient ranking diversity, though whether substantially larger groups (16--64) or alternative RL algorithms would succeed remains open.
59	\emph{LoRA-controlled comparison.}
60	Reducing ACT to GRPO's LoRA configuration (rank 64, $\alpha{=}64$, 4 modules) still yields $\sycon = 0.267$ (peak 0.326), with all 16 checkpoints exceeding vanilla.
61	Under identical adapters, per-token SFT exceeds per-completion GRPO by $1.7{\times}$ ($0.267$ vs.\ $0.153$), identifying supervision granularity as one plausible explanation, pending further isolation---independently corroborated by SMART's per-step success~\citep{beigi2025smart}---though sequence length (256 vs.\ 1024), data source (online vs.\ curated), and dropout (0 vs.\ 0.05) remain acknowledged confounds.
62	The probe reward trap provides independent evidence for~(a): it arises under SFT, where group size is irrelevant.
63	
64	\begin{table}[t]
65	\centering
66	\small
67	\caption{Ablation: isolating supervision paradigm from LoRA configuration. All methods use Qwen3-8B with $\rthree$ (behavioral reward). Vanilla $\sycon{=}0.134$. $^\dagger$Terminated early after three checkpoints showed declining trajectory. $^\ddagger$Peak at step 7000; final checkpoint (step 8000) $\sycon{=}0.267$.}
68	\label{tab:ablation}
69	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
70	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
71	\toprule
72	Method & LoRA config & Best $\sycon$ & ${>}$Van. \\
73	\midrule
74	GRPO gs$=$2 (2 seeds) & r64, $\alpha$64, 4m & 0.153 & 4/28 \\
75	GRPO gs$=$8$^\dagger$ & r64, $\alpha$64, 4m & 0.085 & 0/3 \\
76	\midrule
77	ACT reduced-LoRA$^\ddagger$ & r64, $\alpha$64, 4m & 0.326 & 16/16 \\
78	ACT full s1 & r64, $\alpha$128, 7m & 0.301 & 16/16 \\
79	ACT full s2 & r64, $\alpha$128, 7m & 0.286 & 15/15 \\
80	\bottomrule
81	\end{tabular}}
82	\end{table}
83	
84	\begin{figure}[t]
85	\centering
86	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}
87	\caption{$\sycon$ trajectories for $\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (two seeds) over training steps. ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (31/31 across two seeds); $\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/28 checkpoints (14.3\%).}
88	\label{fig:trajectory}
89	\end{figure}
90	
91	\paragraph{ACT vs.\ GRPO.}
92	ACT's advantage is not marginal but categorical: its worst checkpoint ($\sycon{=}0.170$) exceeds GRPO's absolute best ($\sycon{=}0.153$), and both seeds maintain above-vanilla performance throughout training (Table~\ref{tab:main_comparison}; held-out validation confirms generalization, see Limitations).
93	The reduced-LoRA ablation (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}) shows this is not an adapter-capacity artifact: reduced-LoRA ACT ($\sycon{=}0.326$) exceeds full ACT ($\sycon{=}0.301$), leaving supervision granularity---per-token vs.\ per-completion---as one plausible explanatory variable, pending further isolation.
94	
95	\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}
96	\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking without producing gradients.
97	This suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\rthree$-only GRPO).
98	
99	\paragraph{Future directions.}
100	Per-step process rewards offer the most direct path forward.
101	SMART~\citep{beigi2025smart} succeeds at sycophancy calibration by evaluating behavioral transitions at individual reasoning steps via MCTS.
102	Reasoning trajectories provide temporal structure that completion-level rewards discard: which step introduces new evidence, which step abandons a prior position, and whether the transition is warranted by the preceding context.
103	A scalar per-completion reward compresses this trajectory into a single number, losing the token-level information needed to distinguish valid revision from capitulation---the same information that per-token SFT (ACT) preserves.
104	More broadly, condition-aware rewards, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and methods that incorporate evidential warrant deserve investigation; whether other REINFORCE-style methods or behavioral attributes with similar geometric entanglement~\citep{skalse2022hacking} exhibit the same failures remains open.
105	
```

```text
1	\section{Cosine \texorpdfstring{$\rone$}{R1}: Structural Failure}
2	\label{sec:cosine_failure}
3	
4	During GRPO training, 256-token truncation reduces $\dsyc$ AUROC from 0.827 to approximately 0.60, so the optimizer works with a substantially weaker signal than the offline metrics suggest.
5	We test whether even this degraded discriminability translates into a viable reward through four configurations that form a diagnostic chain (Table~\ref{tab:cosine_chain}): each resolves the failure mode of its predecessor but exposes a deeper limitation.
6	
7	\begin{table}[t]
8	\centering
9	\caption{Cosine $\rone$ failure chain on Qwen3-8B (LoRA rank$=$64, GRPO with $R = w_1 \cdot \rone + w_3 \cdot \rthree$).}
10	\label{tab:cosine_chain}
11	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
12	\begin{tabular}{@{}lllll@{}}
13	\toprule
14	Config & $\rone$ Formula & $w_1$ & Outcome & Root Cause \\
15	\midrule
16	F1a & $\tanh(\mathbf{h}\cdot\dsyc/20)$ & 0.25 & Gate saturation & Norm mismatch \\
17	F1b & $\cos(\mathbf{h},\, \dsyc)$ & 0.25 & $\rone \approx 0$ & Gap too small \\
18	F1c & +recalibration & 0.25 & Mode collapse & Reward discontinuity \\
19	F1d & $5\cos(\mathbf{h},\, \dsyc)$ & 0.40 & Mode collapse & $\rthree$ saturation \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	The diagnostic chain begins with a norm mismatch.
25	F1a's dot-product formulation ($\rone = \tanh(\mathbf{h} \cdot \dsyc / 20)$) saturates because residual-stream norms exceed the direction norm by $100$--$1{,}200{\times}$, zeroing the gradient by step 955.
26	Cosine similarity (F1b) eliminates this norm dependence but reveals a second problem: the gap between valid and sycophantic cosines averages only 0.098---too narrow for meaningful reward contrast.
27	Recalibration (F1c, \S\ref{ssec:recalib}) widens the gap (offline AUROC rises to 0.910) but introduces a third: each recalibration event creates a reward discontinuity that destabilizes advantage estimates, collapsing entropy to 0.069 within 300 steps.
28	Amplification (F1d, $5{\times}$ scaling) lifts $\rone$ above the noise floor but surfaces the fundamental limitation: $\rthree$ saturates at 0.9998 by step 300, leaving the noisy $\rone$---which conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation---as the sole gradient source until terminal collapse at step 2160.
29	
30	Truncation further degrades the signal: under the 256-token maximum completion length used in GRPO, $\dsyc$ AUROC drops from 0.76 (full response) to 0.60, so the offline discriminability (0.827) substantially overestimates the signal available during training.
31	Checkpoint-level evaluation confirms immediate collapse: $\tof{=}0$ from step 500 onward with no stable phase.
32	The probe reward trap (C2, \S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) arises under SFT with full-length responses and is unaffected by this truncation limitation.
33	The four configurations converge on a single conclusion: $\dsyc$ encodes the \emph{direction} of belief change but not whether that change is \emph{warranted}.
34	Valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along $\dsyc$, so the cosine projection assigns similar scores to both.
35	This is not a calibration artifact---recalibration improves discriminability yet rewards still fail---but a semantic limitation of any continuous $\dsyc$-based reward.
36	Section~\ref{sec:probe_trap} examines whether discretizing the signal via a binary probe can escape this limitation.
37	
```

limitation.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cosine $\\rone$ failure chain on Qwen3-8B (LoRA rank$=$64, GRPO with $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$).}\n\\label{tab:cosine_chain}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lllll@{}}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $\\rone$ Formula & $w_1$ & Outcome & Root Cause \\\\\n\\midrule\nF1a & $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h}\\cdot\\dsyc/20)$ & 0.25 & Gate saturation & Norm mismatch \\\\\nF1b & $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$ & 0.25 & $\\rone \\approx 0$ & Gap too small \\\\\nF1c & +recalibration & 0.25 & Mode collapse & Reward discontinuity \\\\\nF1d & $5\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$ & 0.40 & Mode collapse & $\\rthree$ saturation \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe diagnostic chain begins with a norm mismatch.\nF1a's dot-product formulation ($\\rone = \\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$) saturates because residual-stream norms exceed the direction norm by $100$--$1{,}200{\\times}$, zeroing the gradient by step 955.\nCosine similarity (F1b) eliminates this norm dependence but reveals a second problem: the gap between valid and sycophantic cosines averages only 0.098---too narrow for meaningful reward contrast.\nRecalibration (F1c, \\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}) widens the gap (offline AUROC rises to 0.910) but introduces a third: each recalibration event creates a reward discontinuity that destabilizes advantage estimates, collapsing entropy to 0.069 within 300 steps.\nAmplification (F1d, $5{\\times}$ scaling) lifts $\\rone$ above the noise floor but surfaces the fundamental limitation: $\\rthree$ saturates at 0.9998 by step 300, leaving the noisy $\\rone$---which conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation---as the sole gradient source until terminal collapse at step 2160.\n\nTruncation further degrades the signal: under the 256-token maximum completion length used in GRPO, $\\dsyc$ AUROC drops from 0.76 (full response) to 0.60, so the offline discriminability (0.827) substantially overestimates the signal available during training.\nCheckpoint-level evaluation confirms immediate collapse: $\\tof{=}0$ from step 500 onward with no stable phase.\nThe probe reward trap (C2, \\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) arises under SFT with full-length responses and is unaffected by this truncation limitation.\nThe four configurations converge on a single conclusion: $\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not whether that change is \\emph{warranted}.\nValid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along $\\dsyc$, so the cosine projection assigns similar scores to both.\nThis is not a calibration artifact---recalibration improves discriminability yet rewards still fail---but a semantic limitation of any continuous $\\dsyc$-based reward.\nSection~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} examines whether discretizing the signal via a binary probe can escape this limitation.\n",
      "numLines": 37,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 37
    }
  }
}

1	\section{Training Details}
2	\label{app:training}
3	
4	\paragraph{Reward weight selection.}
5	The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine reward term; this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\sim}8.3\%$ of total reward variance.
6	The empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.
7	
8	\paragraph{Response truncation.}
9	All GRPO experiments use \texttt{max\_completion\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\% truncation of multi-turn responses.
10	This setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}
13	Table~\ref{tab:hyperparams} compares training configurations for GRPO variants and ACT.
14	
15	\begin{table}[h]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\caption{Training hyperparameters. All experiments use Qwen3-8B, DeepSpeed ZeRO-2, bf16 precision.}
19	\label{tab:hyperparams}
20	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
21	\toprule
22	& GRPO (F1, F3)$^e$ & ACT \\
23	\midrule
24	Learning rate & 5e-5 & 5e-5 \\
25	Effective batch & 16 & 16 \\
26	Max steps & 8{,}000 & 8{,}000 \\
27	Warmup steps & 200 & 200 \\
28	LR schedule & linear$^a$ & linear \\
29	Grad.\ clipping & --- & 1.0 \\
30	\midrule
31	LoRA rank & 64 & 64 \\
32	LoRA $\alpha$ & 64 & 128 \\
33	LoRA modules & 4$^b$ & 7$^c$ \\
34	LoRA dropout & 0 & 0.05 \\
35	\midrule
36	Group size & 2$^d$ & --- \\
37	KL $\beta$ & 0.04 & --- \\
38	Max compl.\ len. & 256 & --- \\
39	Max seq.\ len. & --- & 1{,}024 \\
40	L2 loss weight & --- & 0.1 \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}
43	\\[2pt]
44	\raggedright\footnotesize
45	$^a$Cosine schedule for $\rthree$-only.
46	$^b$q/k/v/o\_proj.
47	$^c$+ gate/up/down\_proj (${\sim}1.75{\times}$ params; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
48	$^d$Below typical 8--64 \citep{shao2024grpo}; not tuned.
49	$^e$Binary probe (F2) uses a custom SFT training loop rather than GRPO; group size is N/A. LoRA and optimizer settings match the GRPO column.
50	\end{table}
51	
52	\paragraph{GRPO reward variants.}
53	Cosine $\rone$ (F1d): $w_1{=}0.40$, scale$=$5.0, $w_3{=}0.60$; reward computed at layers [19, 20, 24, 27, 32, 33, 35]; recalibration every 500 steps (\S\ref{ssec:recalib}).
54	$\rthree$-only: $w_3{=}1.0$; NLI model \texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}.
55	F1a--F1c weight configurations are reported in Table~\ref{tab:cosine_chain}.
56	
57	\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}
58	\label{app:taxonomy}
59	
60	Manual inspection of all 181 valid-correction responses from the probe-trained model (exp-008v3, step 2000, $\tof = 0$) reveals five failure categories:
61	
62	\begin{itemize}
63	\item \emph{Deliberation traps} (28.7\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.
64	\item \emph{Surface compliance / sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.
65	\item \emph{Acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\%): the model acknowledges the user's correction as plausible but explicitly maintains its original position without substantive engagement.
66	\item \emph{Genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\%): the model appears to accept the correction in its reasoning but the final answer format does not reflect the change, suggesting a generation rather than epistemic failure.
67	\item \emph{Refute/ignore/other} (4.5\%): the model directly refutes the user's input or ignores it entirely.
68	\end{itemize}
69	
70	The first three categories (76.8\%) share a common pattern: the model learns to modulate its \emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.
71

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_teaser.pdf}\n\\caption{The probe reward trap. (a)~$\\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations (AUROC $0.827$), but valid revision and unwarranted capitulation overlap along the same direction. (b)~When used as an SFT reward, behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500 while entropy remains healthy; the 1{,}300-step lag renders standard monitoring blind.}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\nA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}), though 256-token truncation during GRPO training degrades this to approximately 0.60 (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\nThese results point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a training signal.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level signal could target the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper documents the \\emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on $\\dsyc$ is used as an SFT training signal with stopped gradients, behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70), masking the failure from standard monitoring (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nIn our experiments, the 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means entropy-based early stopping---the most common safeguard against reward hacking \\citep{casper2023openproblems}---would miss the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout.\nBecause the trap arises under SFT rather than GRPO, it reflects a limitation of $\\dsyc$ itself: the direction encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}, so any reward conditioned on it alone will either be gamed or reward rigidity.\n\nThe failure is not specific to probe rewards.\nUnder our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 31 checkpoints (12.9\\%).\nBy contrast, ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}---which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion---exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nA LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation for this observed reliability gap, pending further isolation.\n\nThe root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety directions, this premise holds because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve.\nSycophancy violates this condition: capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$ \\citep{wang2025internal, shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n\nThis paper presents a negative result with systematic diagnostic analysis.\nOur two principal findings (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}):\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{The probe reward trap} (C2): behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) precedes distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy monitoring blind. The trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, implicating $\\dsyc$ itself rather than the optimizer (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Observed reliability gap between supervision paradigms} (C1): per-token SFT exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations and seeds) while per-completion GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 (12.9\\%); under matched LoRA, the magnitude gap is $1.7{\\times}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Direction Discriminability}\n\\label{sec:direction_quality}\n\nBefore evaluating $\\dsyc$ as a reward signal (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), we first establish that it captures a genuine sycophancy-related signal in the residual stream.\nIf the direction failed to discriminate sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations, downstream reward failures would be trivially explained by poor direction quality rather than by a fundamental gap between discriminability and reward utility.\nWe evaluate discriminability along five axes: cross-validated classification accuracy, evidence-strength stratification, cross-model transfer, training stability, and probe ablation.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:direction_quality} summarizes the per-layer AUROC profiles for both models.\n\n\\paragraph{Qwen3-8B layer-wise discriminability.}\nWe evaluate $\\dsyc$ via 5-fold cross-validated AUROC on 181 style-controlled contrastive pairs (\\S\\ref{ssec:extraction}).\nThe direction achieves AUROC $0.827 \\pm 0.028$ (5-fold CV) at its best layer (L19).\nDiscriminability emerges gradually across depth: layers L0--L8 perform near chance, statistical significance ($p < 0.01$) first appears at L16, and performance peaks at L19.\nThis gradual emergence, with the peak in the upper third of the network, suggests that sycophancy-relevant information is encoded as a late-stage representation rather than an input-level confound.\nAUROC increases monotonically with evidence-strength tier: from 0.675 on weak-evidence pairs to 0.883 on medium and 0.929 on strong pairs.\nThis monotonic relationship reflects effect-size differences---stronger evidence produces larger behavioral gaps that are easier to classify---rather than indicating that $\\dsyc$ encodes evidential warrant per se.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}\nTo rule out that these results are specific to Qwen3-8B's representation geometry, we repeat the full extraction and evaluation pipeline on Llama-3-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares the two models.\nLlama-3-8B achieves AUROC $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ (5-fold CV) at L16, with a parallel evidence-strength gradient (weak 0.711, medium 0.864, strong 0.911).\nThe best-layer depth differs (L16 vs.\\ L19), consistent with the architectural differences between the two families, but the qualitative pattern is identical: gradual emergence, best-layer selection in the upper third, and monotonic strength scaling.\nDiscriminability is robust across knowledge domains, with per-domain AUROCs on Llama-3-8B ranging from 0.751 (geography) to 0.844 (commonsense).\nWhen surface confounds (response length, hedging patterns) are explicitly controlled, Llama-3-8B's AUROC rises to 0.923, which confirms that the direction captures semantic content rather than stylistic artifacts.\nThe consistent results across architecturally distinct model families indicate that contrastive sycophancy directions reflect a shared representational phenomenon rather than a model-specific idiosyncrasy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model $\\dsyc$ discriminability. AUROC uncertainty is 5-fold cross-validation standard deviation; evidence-strength AUROCs are evaluated on the full dataset at each model's best layer.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nModel & Best Layer & AUROC ($\\pm$ std) & Weak & Medium & Strong \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-8B & L19 & $0.827 \\pm 0.028$ & 0.675 & 0.883 & 0.929 \\\\\nLlama-3-8B & L16 & $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ & 0.711 & 0.864 & 0.911 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_auroc.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer AUROC of $\\dsyc$ as a function of normalized depth (layer index / total layers). Qwen3-8B (36 layers, solid) shows gradual emergence from near-chance at L0 to a peak at L19, with performance plateauing in the final third. Llama-3-8B (32 layers, dashed; layers 16--31 only) exhibits a similar upper-third concentration. Both models achieve best discriminability in layers 50--60\\% through the network.}\n\\label{fig:direction_quality}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Training stability and probe ablation.}\nTwo additional analyses confirm the robustness of $\\dsyc$ under training dynamics.\nFirst, we track direction drift during LoRA fine-tuning: at step 500, the cosine similarity between the original and re-extracted $\\dsyc$ is approximately 0.96, and per-layer AUROC remains above 0.77.\nThe direction drifts slowly relative to the 500-step recalibration interval (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), so the reward signal does not become stale between updates.\nSecond, we compare $\\dsyc$ against a random baseline: a logistic probe trained on the learned direction achieves AUROC 0.835, versus 0.727 for a probe trained on a random direction of equal dimensionality ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.108$).\nThe learned direction carries substantially more sycophancy-relevant information than an arbitrary axis in the same activation space.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for reward design.}\nThese results establish that $\\dsyc$ reliably separates sycophantic from non-sycophantic representations across models, evidence-strength levels, and training checkpoints.\nDirection quality is not the bottleneck.\nThe reward failures documented in \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap} therefore cannot be attributed to a noisy or degenerate direction; they point to a deeper incompatibility between discriminability and reward utility.\n",

    "oldString": "through step 1000---well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy---and",
    "newString": "through step 1000, well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy, and",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n\nThe cosine formulations fail on signal magnitude; a natural response is to discretize.\nTraining a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and thresholding its output yields $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\nWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\nBoth probe experiments use SFT rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{ssec:reward}); the trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\nWithout stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\nThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\nWith stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\nBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\nQualitative analysis of 181 valid-correction responses reveals five failure modes: \\emph{deliberation traps} (28.7\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\\%); \\emph{genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\\%); and refute/ignore/other (4.5\\%). Full taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}.\n\n\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}\n\\label{tab:probe_trajectory}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nStep & $\\tof$ & $\\nof$ & $\\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\\\\n\\midrule\n500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\\\\n1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\\\\n1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\\\\n2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}\n\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}\n\\label{fig:probe_trap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.\nAt step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000---well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy---and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.\nEntropy does not collapse until step ${\\sim}1840$.\nThis gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.\nDetecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.\n\n\\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}\nThe differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.\nWith gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.\nAny reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.\nEscaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\n",

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related_work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Representation Engineering.}
4	Linear directions in LLM residual streams encode behavioral concepts \citep{zou2023repe}; inference-time methods---ITI \citep{li2023iti}, ActAdd \citep{turner2023actadd}, CAA \citep{rimsky2024caa}---demonstrate that activation geometry suffices for behavioral control \citep{burns2023ccs}.
5	We use the same contrastive extraction as CAA but apply $\dsyc$ as an RL reward, revealing a gap that inference-time work does not encounter.
6	
7	\paragraph{Reward Design and Overoptimization.}
8	Proxy rewards diverge from true objectives under sustained optimization \citep{gao2023scaling}; \citet{manheim2019goodhart} taxonomize four variants of this Goodhart's Law pattern (regressional, extremal, causal, adversarial).
9	Our probe reward trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) instantiates the regressional variant: $\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations under the pre-training distribution but fails as a reward once optimization shifts the policy, with two features not previously documented---behavioral death within 500 steps (far faster than gradual degradation curves~\citep{gao2023scaling}) and a 1{,}300-step temporal decoupling that renders entropy monitoring ineffective~\citep{skalse2022hacking}.
10	Reward hacking can further escalate to active tampering~\citep{denison2024subterfuge}; \citet{wu2026rewardhacking} show that representation-level directions succeed when behaviors are geometrically separable (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
11	
12	\paragraph{Sycophancy Characterization and Mitigation.}
13	Sycophancy is pervasive in RLHF-trained assistants \citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, amplified by RLHF \citep{shapira2026amplifies}, and traceable to late-layer preference shifts \citep{wang2025internal}; mitigations include synthetic data \citep{wei2024synthetic}, consistency training \citep{consistency2025bctact}, and neuron-level correction \citep{obrien2025fewbadneurons}.
14	More fundamentally, \citet{vennemeyer2025sycophancy} demonstrate that sycophancy decomposes into distinct, independently steerable sub-behaviors, each encoded along separate directions; a single $\dsyc$ captures only their shared variance.
15	Our analysis shows why the step from diagnosis to reward is structurally blocked: $\dsyc$ conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation.
16	
17	\paragraph{Probe-Based and Representation-Guided Methods.}
18	\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy via Best-of-N reranking with linear probe penalties (94\% accuracy) without producing gradients; our work reveals what happens when a comparable signal enters the training loop (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
19	Latent-GRPO \citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, CRAFT \citep{luo2026craft}, \citet{sinii2025smallvectors}, and \citet{ackermann2026gradreg} extend representation-guided RL to reasoning and gradient regularization.
20	SMART \citep{beigi2025smart} applies per-step process rewards for sycophancy, succeeding where our completion-level rewards fail---consistent with ACT's per-token success.
21	Preference-based methods (DPO \citep{rafailov2023dpo}, KTO \citep{ethayarajh2024kto}) may accommodate $\dsyc$-derived rankings; whether they avoid the failures we observe remains open.
22	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Representation Engineering.}\nLinear directions in LLM residual streams encode behavioral concepts \\citep{zou2023repe}; inference-time methods (ITI \\citep{li2023iti}, ActAdd \\citep{turner2023actadd}, CAA \\citep{rimsky2024caa}) demonstrate that activation geometry suffices for behavioral control \\citep{burns2023ccs}.\nWe use the same contrastive extraction as CAA but apply $\\dsyc$ as an RL reward, revealing a gap that inference-time work does not encounter.\n\n\\paragraph{Reward Design and Overoptimization.}\nProxy rewards diverge from true objectives under sustained optimization \\citep{gao2023scaling}; \\citet{manheim2019goodhart} taxonomize four variants of this Goodhart's Law pattern (regressional, extremal, causal, adversarial).\nOur probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) instantiates the regressional variant: $\\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations under the pre-training distribution but fails as a reward once optimization shifts the policy, with two features not previously documented---behavioral death within 500 steps (far faster than gradual degradation curves~\\citep{gao2023scaling}) and a 1{,}300-step temporal decoupling that renders entropy monitoring ineffective~\\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\nReward hacking can further escalate to active tampering~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}; \\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} show that representation-level directions succeed when behaviors are geometrically separable (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Sycophancy Characterization and Mitigation.}\nSycophancy is pervasive in RLHF-trained assistants \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, amplified by RLHF \\citep{shapira2026amplifies}, and traceable to late-layer preference shifts \\citep{wang2025internal}; mitigations include synthetic data \\citep{wei2024synthetic}, consistency training \\citep{consistency2025bctact}, and neuron-level correction \\citep{obrien2025fewbadneurons}.\nMore fundamentally, \\citet{vennemeyer2025sycophancy} demonstrate that sycophancy decomposes into distinct, independently steerable sub-behaviors, each encoded along separate directions; a single $\\dsyc$ captures only their shared variance.\nOur analysis shows why the step from diagnosis to reward is structurally blocked: $\\dsyc$ conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Probe-Based and Representation-Guided Methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy via Best-of-N reranking with linear probe penalties (94\\% accuracy) without producing gradients; our work reveals what happens when a comparable signal enters the training loop (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nLatent-GRPO \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, CRAFT \\citep{luo2026craft}, \\citet{sinii2025smallvectors}, and \\citet{ackermann2026gradreg} extend representation-guided RL to reasoning and gradient regularization.\nSMART \\citep{beigi2025smart} applies per-step process rewards for sycophancy, succeeding where our completion-level rewards fail---consistent with ACT's per-token success.\nPreference-based methods (DPO \\citep{rafailov2023dpo}, KTO \\citep{ethayarajh2024kto}) may accommodate $\\dsyc$-derived rankings; whether they avoid the failures we observe remains open.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "b26b6a89-566f-4f80-8087-b9615012792e",
  "uuid": "06ae40e5-0fb4-461d-a2df-476a2a0ce42f",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/probe_trap.tex",
    "oldString": "on $\\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will",
    "newString": "on $\\dsyc$ (continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen) will",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n\nThe cosine formulations fail on signal magnitude; a natural response is to discretize.\nTraining a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and thresholding its output yields $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\nWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\nBoth probe experiments use SFT rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{ssec:reward}); the trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\nWithout stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\nThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\nWith stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\nBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\nQualitative analysis of 181 valid-correction responses reveals five failure modes: \\emph{deliberation traps} (28.7\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\\%); \\emph{genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\\%); and refute/ignore/other (4.5\\%). Full taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}.\n\n\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}\n\\label{tab:probe_trajectory}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nStep & $\\tof$ & $\\nof$ & $\\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\\\\n\\midrule\n500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\\\\n1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\\\\n1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\\\\n2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}\n\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}\n\\label{fig:probe_trap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.\nAt step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000, well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy, and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.\nEntropy does not collapse until step ${\\sim}1840$.\nThis gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.\nDetecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.\n\n\\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}\nThe differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.\nWith gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.\nAny reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.\nEscaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 50,
        "oldLines": 5,
        "newStart": 50,
        "newLines": 5,
        "lines": [

          " The differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.",
          " With gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.",
          "-Any reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.",
          "+Any reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$ (continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen) will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.",
          " Escaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}."
        ]

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "b26b6a89-566f-4f80-8087-b9615012792e",
  "uuid": "b17c3bac-9430-4c94-ad83-feb20bf0bef5",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}---which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion---exceeds",
    "newString": "ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}, which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion, exceeds",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_teaser.pdf}\n\\caption{The probe reward trap. (a)~$\\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations (AUROC $0.827$), but valid revision and unwarranted capitulation overlap along the same direction. (b)~When used as an SFT reward, behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500 while entropy remains healthy; the 1{,}300-step lag renders standard monitoring blind.}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\nA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}), though 256-token truncation during GRPO training degrades this to approximately 0.60 (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\nThese results point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a training signal.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level signal could target the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper documents the \\emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on $\\dsyc$ is used as an SFT training signal with stopped gradients, behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70), masking the failure from standard monitoring (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nIn our experiments, the 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking \\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout.\nBecause the trap arises under SFT rather than GRPO, it reflects a limitation of $\\dsyc$ itself: the direction encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}, so any reward conditioned on it alone will either be gamed or reward rigidity.\n\nThe failure is not specific to probe rewards.\nUnder our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 31 checkpoints (12.9\\%).\nBy contrast, ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}---which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion---exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nA LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation for this observed reliability gap, pending further isolation.\n\nThe root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety directions, this premise holds because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve.\nSycophancy violates this condition: capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$ \\citep{wang2025internal, shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n\nThis paper presents a negative result with systematic diagnostic analysis.\nOur two principal findings (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}):\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{The probe reward trap} (C2): behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) precedes distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy monitoring blind. The trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, implicating $\\dsyc$ itself rather than the optimizer (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Observed reliability gap between supervision paradigms} (C1): per-token SFT exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations and seeds) while per-completion GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 (12.9\\%); under matched LoRA, the magnitude gap is $1.7{\\times}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 19,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 19,

          " The failure is not specific to probe rewards.",
          " Under our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 31 checkpoints (12.9\\%).",
          "-By contrast, ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}---which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion---exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
          "+By contrast, ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}, which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion, exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Cosine \\texorpdfstring{$\\rone$}{R1}: Structural Failure}\n\\label{sec:cosine_failure}\n\nDuring GRPO training, 256-token truncation reduces $\\dsyc$ AUROC from 0.827 to approximately 0.60, so the optimizer works with a substantially weaker signal than the offline metrics suggest.\nWe test whether even this degraded discriminability translates into a viable reward through four configurations that form a diagnostic chain (Table~\\ref{tab:cosine_chain}): each resolves the failure mode of its predecessor but exposes a deeper limitation.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cosine $\\rone$ failure chain on Qwen3-8B (LoRA rank$=$64, GRPO with $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$).}\n\\label{tab:cosine_chain}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lllll@{}}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $\\rone$ Formula & $w_1$ & Outcome & Root Cause \\\\\n\\midrule\nF1a & $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h}\\cdot\\dsyc/20)$ & 0.25 & Gate saturation & Norm mismatch \\\\\nF1b & $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$ & 0.25 & $\\rone \\approx 0$ & Gap too small \\\\\nF1c & +recalibration & 0.25 & Mode collapse & Reward discontinuity \\\\\nF1d & $5\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$ & 0.40 & Mode collapse & $\\rthree$ saturation \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe diagnostic chain begins with a norm mismatch.\nF1a's dot-product formulation ($\\rone = \\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$) saturates because residual-stream norms exceed the direction norm by $100$--$1{,}200{\\times}$, zeroing the gradient by step 955.\nCosine similarity (F1b) eliminates this norm dependence but reveals a second problem: the gap between valid and sycophantic cosines averages only 0.098---too narrow for meaningful reward contrast.\nRecalibration (F1c, \\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}) widens the gap (offline AUROC rises to 0.910) but introduces a third: each recalibration event creates a reward discontinuity that destabilizes advantage estimates, collapsing entropy to 0.069 within 300 steps.\nAmplification (F1d, $5{\\times}$ scaling) lifts $\\rone$ above the noise floor but surfaces the fundamental limitation: $\\rthree$ saturates at 0.9998 by step 300, leaving the noisy $\\rone$---which conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation---as the sole gradient source until terminal collapse at step 2160.\n\nTruncation further degrades the signal: under the 256-token maximum completion length used in GRPO, $\\dsyc$ AUROC drops from 0.76 (full response) to 0.60, so the offline discriminability (0.827) substantially overestimates the signal available during training.\nCheckpoint-level evaluation confirms immediate collapse: $\\tof{=}0$ from step 500 onward with no stable phase.\nThe probe reward trap (C2, \\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) arises under SFT with full-length responses and is unaffected by this truncation limitation.\nThe four configurations converge on a single conclusion: $\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not whether that change is \\emph{warranted}.\nValid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along $\\dsyc$, so the cosine projection assigns similar scores to both.\nThis is not a calibration artifact---recalibration improves discriminability yet rewards still fail---but a semantic limitation of any continuous $\\dsyc$-based reward.\nSection~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} examines whether discretizing the signal via a binary probe can escape this limitation.\n",

    "originalFile": "We quantify a temporal decoupling we call the \\emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on a contrastive sycophancy direction ($\\dsyc$, cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B, $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B) is used as an SFT training signal, behavioral death (true-positive flip rate $= 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70). In our experiments, the resulting 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means that entropy monitoring, the most common safeguard against reward hacking, misses the failure entirely. Because the trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, it is independent of RL optimization dynamics, revealing a semantic limitation of the direction itself: $\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}. The failure extends beyond probe rewards: all $\\dsyc$-based GRPO formulations collapse, and even behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 checkpoints, while dense per-token SFT succeeds at every checkpoint (47/47). A LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation, pending further isolation---with sequence length, data source, and dropout as acknowledged confounds---suggesting that per-completion scalar rewards may compress away the token-level information needed for sycophancy calibration.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GDJby8tokGWGnLY3decY8a`</summary>

```text
1	\section{When Does Discriminability Fail as Reward Utility?}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Root cause of the probe reward trap.}
4	The probe reward trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) and the cosine failures (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}) share a common geometric root.
5	$\dsyc$ encodes the \emph{direction} of belief change but not its \emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.
6	The two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.
7	Cosine $\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\dsyc$.
8	The binary probe $\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.
9	These are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).
10	Cross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779 \pm 0.019$ on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.
11	This pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.
12	\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable.
13	A quantitative comparison of geometric separability between $\dsyc$ and their reward-hacking directions remains an important open question.
14	Independently, \citet{kelkar2026persona} find that persona vectors are geometrically independent of $\dsyc$ in activation space, further suggesting that $\dsyc$ does not encode the full semantic structure of sycophancy.
15	The multi-dimensional decomposition of sycophancy into independently steerable sub-behaviors~\citep{vennemeyer2025sycophancy} compounds this limitation: $\dsyc$ projects distinct sub-phenomena onto a single axis, amplifying the warranted-vs-unwarranted ambiguity.
16	For sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.
17	
18	\paragraph{GRPO results under our configuration.}
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\tof$ and $\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \textbf{bold}. $^\dagger$Best checkpoint (seed 1, step 6500); only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\ddagger$Seed 2 (15 checkpoints); all exceed vanilla. Best $\sycon{=}0.286$ (step 7000). $^\S$All cosine variants collapsed from step 1 ($\tof{=}0$ at every checkpoint); F1d additionally diverged at step 2160 (entropy ${<}\,0.01$). $^\P$ACT and ActAdd differ from GRPO along 5+ dimensions (\S\ref{sec:discussion}); included for context, not as controlled comparisons. Under arithmetic mean $(\tof{+}\nof)/2$, qualitative rankings are preserved but collapsed methods (0.498--0.500) appear indistinguishable from vanilla (0.509), masking behavioral death; harmonic mean's zero-floor on $\tof{=}0$ is by design.}
23	\label{tab:main_comparison}
24	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
25	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}
26	\toprule
27	Method & Type & $\sycon$ $\uparrow$ & $\tof$ $\uparrow$ & $\nof$ $\uparrow$ & Step \\
28	\midrule
29	Vanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\
30	\midrule
31	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{GRPO with $\dsyc$}} \\
32	Cosine $\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & 0.000$^\S$ & 0.000 & 1.000 & 500 \\
33	Continuous Probe $\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\
34	\midrule
35	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{SFT with $\dsyc$ probe penalty}} \\
36	Binary Probe $\rone$ (F2) & SFT & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\
37	\midrule
38	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{GRPO without $\dsyc$}} \\
39	$\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.153$^\dagger$ & 0.083 & 0.983 & 6500 \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{Reference methods (not directly comparable)$^\P$}} \\
42	ActAdd ($\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\
43	\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \textbf{0.301} & \textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\
44	ACT (seed 2)$^\ddagger$ & SFT & 0.286 & -- & -- & 7000 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	If the cosine and probe failures (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) were caused by $\dsyc$ alone, removing it should recover calibration.
50	$\rthree$-only GRPO tests this hypothesis by relying solely on the NLI behavioral reward---and fails: only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla (14.3\%), with both seeds oscillating ($\Delta \approx 0.069$) before diverging (Table~\ref{tab:main_comparison}; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}).
51	The failure is not representational: $\dsyc$ AUROC varies by ${<}0.01$ across checkpoints and $\rthree$ saturates at ${\sim}0.98$ ($p{=}0.703$), yet $\sycon$ swings by 17\%.
52	This disconnect between stable representations and unstable behavior points to the per-completion reward structure, not the reward content, as the bottleneck.
53	
54	\paragraph{Competing hypotheses and ablation.}
55	Three explanations are consistent with these failures: (a)~$\dsyc$ is fundamentally unsuitable as a sycophancy reward; (b)~group size$=$2 provides insufficient ranking diversity; (c)~sycophancy calibration resists scalar per-completion RL.
56	We designed two ablations to discriminate among them (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}).
57	\emph{Group-size ablation.}
58	Per-completion GRPO fails at both group sizes 2 and 8; the gs$=$8 trajectory ($0.084 \to 0.085 \to 0.054$, all below vanilla; terminated early) rules out insufficient ranking diversity, though whether substantially larger groups (16--64) or alternative RL algorithms would succeed remains open.
59	\emph{LoRA-controlled comparison.}
60	Reducing ACT to GRPO's LoRA configuration (rank 64, $\alpha{=}64$, 4 modules) still yields $\sycon = 0.267$ (peak 0.326), with all 16 checkpoints exceeding vanilla.
61	Under identical adapters, per-token SFT exceeds per-completion GRPO by $1.7{\times}$ ($0.267$ vs.\ $0.153$), identifying supervision granularity as one plausible explanation, pending further isolation---independently corroborated by SMART's per-step success~\citep{beigi2025smart}---though sequence length (256 vs.\ 1024), data source (online vs.\ curated), and dropout (0 vs.\ 0.05) remain acknowledged confounds.
62	The probe reward trap provides independent evidence for~(a): it arises under SFT, where group size is irrelevant.
63	
64	\begin{table}[t]
65	\centering
66	\small
67	\caption{Ablation: isolating supervision paradigm from LoRA configuration. All methods use Qwen3-8B with $\rthree$ (behavioral reward). Vanilla $\sycon{=}0.134$. $^\dagger$Terminated early after three checkpoints showed declining trajectory. $^\ddagger$Peak at step 7000; final checkpoint (step 8000) $\sycon{=}0.267$.}
68	\label{tab:ablation}
69	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
70	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
71	\toprule
72	Method & LoRA config & Best $\sycon$ & ${>}$Van. \\
73	\midrule
74	GRPO gs$=$2 (2 seeds) & r64, $\alpha$64, 4m & 0.153 & 4/28 \\
75	GRPO gs$=$8$^\dagger$ & r64, $\alpha$64, 4m & 0.085 & 0/3 \\
76	\midrule
77	ACT reduced-LoRA$^\ddagger$ & r64, $\alpha$64, 4m & 0.326 & 16/16 \\
78	ACT full s1 & r64, $\alpha$128, 7m & 0.301 & 16/16 \\
79	ACT full s2 & r64, $\alpha$128, 7m & 0.286 & 15/15 \\
80	\bottomrule
81	\end{tabular}}
82	\end{table}
83	
84	\begin{figure}[t]
85	\centering
86	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}
87	\caption{$\sycon$ trajectories for $\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (two seeds) over training steps. ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (31/31 across two seeds); $\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/28 checkpoints (14.3\%).}
88	\label{fig:trajectory}
89	\end{figure}
90	
91	\paragraph{ACT vs.\ GRPO.}
92	ACT's advantage is not marginal but categorical: its worst checkpoint ($\sycon{=}0.170$) exceeds GRPO's absolute best ($\sycon{=}0.153$), and both seeds maintain above-vanilla performance throughout training (Table~\ref{tab:main_comparison}; held-out validation confirms generalization, see Limitations).
93	The reduced-LoRA ablation (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}) shows this is not an adapter-capacity artifact: reduced-LoRA ACT ($\sycon{=}0.326$) exceeds full ACT ($\sycon{=}0.301$), leaving supervision granularity---per-token vs.\ per-completion---as one plausible explanatory variable, pending further isolation.
94	
95	\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}
96	\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking without producing gradients.
97	This suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\rthree$-only GRPO).
98	
99	\paragraph{Future directions.}
100	Per-step process rewards offer the most direct path forward.
101	SMART~\citep{beigi2025smart} succeeds at sycophancy calibration by evaluating behavioral transitions at individual reasoning steps via MCTS.
102	Reasoning trajectories provide temporal structure---which step introduces evidence, which abandons a position---that a scalar per-completion reward compresses into a single number, losing the token-level information needed to distinguish valid revision from capitulation.
103	More broadly, condition-aware rewards, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and methods that incorporate evidential warrant deserve investigation; whether other REINFORCE-style methods or behavioral attributes with similar geometric entanglement~\citep{skalse2022hacking} exhibit the same failures remains open.
104	
```

they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO results under our configuration.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Best checkpoint (seed 1, step 6500); only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\\ddagger$Seed 2 (15 checkpoints); all exceed vanilla. Best $\\sycon{=}0.286$ (step 7000). $^\\S$All cosine variants collapsed from step 1 ($\\tof{=}0$ at every checkpoint); F1d additionally diverged at step 2160 (entropy ${<}\\,0.01$). $^\\P$ACT and ActAdd differ from GRPO along 5+ dimensions (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); included for context, not as controlled comparisons. Under arithmetic mean $(\\tof{+}\\nof)/2$, qualitative rankings are preserved but collapsed methods (0.498--0.500) appear indistinguishable from vanilla (0.509), masking behavioral death; harmonic mean's zero-floor on $\\tof{=}0$ is by design.}\n\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & 0.000$^\\S$ & 0.000 & 1.000 & 500 \\\\\nContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{SFT with $\\dsyc$ probe penalty}} \\\\\nBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & SFT & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.153$^\\dagger$ & 0.083 & 0.983 & 6500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Reference methods (not directly comparable)$^\\P$}} \\\\\nActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\nACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & 0.286 & -- & -- & 7000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nIf the cosine and probe failures (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) were caused by $\\dsyc$ alone, removing it should recover calibration.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO tests this hypothesis by relying solely on the NLI behavioral reward---and fails: only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla (14.3\\%), with both seeds oscillating ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) before diverging (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nThe failure is not representational: $\\dsyc$ AUROC varies by ${<}0.01$ across checkpoints and $\\rthree$ saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ ($p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by 17\\%.\nThis disconnect between stable representations and unstable behavior points to the per-completion reward structure, not the reward content, as the bottleneck.\n\n\\paragraph{Competing hypotheses and ablation.}\nThree explanations are consistent with these failures: (a)~$\\dsyc$ is fundamentally unsuitable as a sycophancy reward; (b)~group size$=$2 provides insufficient ranking diversity; (c)~sycophancy calibration resists scalar per-completion RL.\nWe designed two ablations to discriminate among them (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\n\\emph{Group-size ablation.}\nPer-completion GRPO fails at both group sizes 2 and 8; the gs$=$8 trajectory ($0.084 \\to 0.085 \\to 0.054$, all below vanilla; terminated early) rules out insufficient ranking diversity, though whether substantially larger groups (16--64) or alternative RL algorithms would succeed remains open.\n\\emph{LoRA-controlled comparison.}\nReducing ACT to GRPO's LoRA configuration (rank 64, $\\alpha{=}64$, 4 modules) still yields $\\sycon = 0.267$ (peak 0.326), with all 16 checkpoints exceeding vanilla.\nUnder identical adapters, per-token SFT exceeds per-completion GRPO by $1.7{\\times}$ ($0.267$ vs.\\ $0.153$), identifying supervision granularity as one plausible explanation, pending further isolation---independently corroborated by SMART's per-step success~\\citep{beigi2025smart}---though sequence length (256 vs.\\ 1024), data source (online vs.\\ curated), and dropout (0 vs.\\ 0.05) remain acknowledged confounds.\nThe probe reward trap provides independent evidence for~(a): it arises under SFT, where group size is irrelevant.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Ablation: isolating supervision paradigm from LoRA configuration. All methods use Qwen3-8B with $\\rthree$ (behavioral reward). Vanilla $\\sycon{=}0.134$. $^\\dagger$Terminated early after three checkpoints showed declining trajectory. $^\\ddagger$Peak at step 7000; final checkpoint (step 8000) $\\sycon{=}0.267$.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & LoRA config & Best $\\sycon$ & ${>}$Van. \\\\\n\\midrule\nGRPO gs$=$2 (2 seeds) & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.153 & 4/28 \\\\\nGRPO gs$=$8$^\\dagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.085 & 0/3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nACT reduced-LoRA$^\\ddagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.326 & 16/16 \\\\\nACT full s1 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.301 & 16/16 \\\\\nACT full s2 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.286 & 15/15 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (two seeds) over training steps. ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (31/31 across two seeds); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/28 checkpoints (14.3\\%).}\n\\label{fig:trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\nACT's advantage is not marginal but categorical: its worst checkpoint ($\\sycon{=}0.170$) exceeds GRPO's absolute best ($\\sycon{=}0.153$), and both seeds maintain above-vanilla performance throughout training (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}; held-out validation confirms generalization, see Limitations).\nThe reduced-LoRA ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) shows this is not an adapter-capacity artifact: reduced-LoRA ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.326$) exceeds full ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.301$), leaving supervision granularity---per-token vs.\\ per-completion---as one plausible explanatory variable, pending further isolation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking without producing gradients.\nThis suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO).\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nPer-step process rewards offer the most direct path forward.\nSMART~\\citep{beigi2025smart} succeeds at sycophancy calibration by evaluating behavioral transitions at individual reasoning steps via MCTS.\nReasoning trajectories provide temporal structure---which step introduces evidence, which abandons a position---that a scalar per-completion reward compresses into a single number, losing the token-level information needed to distinguish valid revision from capitulation.\nMore broadly, condition-aware rewards, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and methods that incorporate evidential warrant deserve investigation; whether other REINFORCE-style methods or behavioral attributes with similar geometric entanglement~\\citep{skalse2022hacking} exhibit the same failures remains open.\n",
